{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01NPXqb8G9PPTFQAr6VPhAm1","type":"tool_result","content":"# Validate tool input against the registered inputSchema\n\nIssue: re-cinq/HALEngine#37\nLabels: priority:medium, type:feature, size:M, area:tools, epic:extension-points\n\nPart of #35\n\n## Why\n\n`ToolRegistry.execute` looks a tool up, logs the input's key names, and calls the executor\nwithout ever checking the declared `inputSchema` (`src/orchestration/tools/registry.ts`).\nEvery tool declares a JSON Schema (`src/types/ai.ts`) that is handed verbatim to both\nproviders, but nothing validates what comes back from the model. The shipped example shows the\nshape of the problem: `example/weather-tool.ts` declares `location` as a required string, then\ncasts and defaults it (`(input.location as string) || 'Unknown'`) with a comment warning the reader\nthat `inputSchema` shapes what the model sends but does not enforce it. That is the careful version,\nwritten by the people who wrote the engine — and it still answers a malformed call with a\nconfidently formatted forecast for `'Unknown'` rather than telling the model it got the call wrong.\n\nThe registry currently pushes this check onto each tool's own executor, leaving every consumer\nto implement it itself — differently each time, or not at all. A tool that receives unexpected input must fail\nin one predictable, known way before the rest of this epic's tool work can be built on top\nof it.\n\n## What to do\n\nValidate `input` against the resolved tool definition's `inputSchema` inside\n`ToolRegistry.execute`, before the executor runs, using a JSON Schema validator (`ajv`\nunless the build proves otherwise) added to `dependencies` — not a second, hand-written\nschema in a different format, since the schema already has to match what is handed to the\nproviders.\n\nCompile a static tool's schema once at `register` time so a broken schema fails at startup,\nnot mid-conversation; a schema produced by a function-valued `ToolDefinitionSource`\nresolves and compiles per call, cached by the resolved schema object.\n\nA rejection returns a `ToolResponse` (`{result: '<what was wrong>'}`) rather than throwing,\nso a malformed call becomes something the model can read and correct within the existing\n`DEFAULT_MAX_TOOL_ROUNDS = 5` budget, instead of ending the whole conversation with a\ngeneric transport error. Validation enforces `required`, declared types and `enum` only —\nit does not inject `additionalProperties: false`, coerce types, or apply defaults, and\nthere is no opt-out flag.\n\nUpdate `docs/adding-a-tool.md`, `README.md` and `.specify/spec.md` to match.\n\n## Acceptance criteria\n\n- [ ] `ToolRegistry.execute` validates `input` against the resolved definition's\n      `inputSchema` before calling the executor; on failure it returns a `ToolResponse`\n      whose `result` describes the failure, and the executor is never invoked\n- [ ] A new `src/orchestration/tools/registry.test.ts` (the registry has no test file today)\n      registers a tool requiring a `location` string, calls `execute` with `{}`, and\n      asserts the returned result names `location` as required and the executor's call\n      count is `0`\n- [ ] A test asserts valid input with an unlisted extra property is passed through to the\n      executor unchanged (`toEqual`, so an added default or coerced value fails the test) —\n      exactly once\n- [ ] A test asserts a wrong-typed field (a number where a string is required) is rejected\n      with a message naming the field path and the expected type, and that the message does\n      **not** contain the offending value\n- [ ] A test asserts `register` throws at registration time for an uncompilable schema, and\n      that the thrown message names the tool\n- [ ] A test asserts a function-valued tool definition whose returned schema differs between\n      calls is validated against the schema current at that call, not a stale cached one\n- [ ] A test asserts a static tool's schema is compiled once across ten `execute` calls\n- [ ] A tool-loop test in `chatOrchestrator.test.ts` drives a stub provider through a\n      rejected tool call followed by a corrected retry, and asserts `processMessage`\n      resolves — not rejects — with the rejection text visible to the provider on the next\n      round\n- [ ] `execute` still throws `Unknown tool: <name>` for an unregistered name, unchanged,\n      pinned by a test\n- [ ] Rejection messages and log lines name the failing property paths and the constraint\n      that failed, and never a property **value**. Tool input is where identifying data\n      enters the engine and the engine redacts nothing today. A test asserts a rejected\n      payload containing an identifying field produces no log line and no returned text\n      containing that field's value\n- [ ] `execute` carries a **one-line** doc comment — `re-lint/max-comment-lines` rejects a\n      `@param`/`@returns`/`@throws` block — and the full contract (rejection `ToolResponse` for input\n      the schema rejects, throw only for an unknown tool name) is stated in\n      `specs/hal-engine-tool-input-validation/spec.md`, which the comment points at\n- [ ] The validator is in `package.json` `dependencies`, not `peerDependencies` or\n      `devDependencies`, and\n      `npm run build && node --input-type=module -e \"import('./dist/index.js')\"` exits `0`\n- [ ] `README.md` states what the registry now does, and the claim is true rather than\n      merely present\n- [ ] `docs/adding-a-tool.md` no longer tells executors to cast defensively and handle\n      missing input; it states that input reaching an executor has already passed the\n      declared schema, that unknown keys may still be present, and that a rejection is\n      returned to the model rather than thrown\n- [ ] `.specify/spec.md` records that a rejection is a tool result (not an exception), that\n      the tool-round bound is what stops a retry cycle from spinning, and that extra\n      properties pass through unmodified\n- [ ] The PR body states the migration note: an executor that used to hand-validate its\n      input now receives only input the schema already accepts, and a tool that relied on\n      seeing malformed input to produce its own error message stops seeing it. The version\n      bump is MINOR\n- [ ] Every test this issue adds cites the statement it validates with an inline\n      `([validated by ...])` link in a `specs/**` or `adrs/**` file, and the statements exist\n      before the tests do. `re-lint/require-spec-link` fails the build on an uncited test, so the\n      spec edits are part of this issue rather than a follow-up\n- [ ] `CHANGELOG.md` carries an entry for this change, written for somebody reading it on npm\n      rather than somebody reading this repo's log. CI runs `scripts/check-changelog.sh` and\n      fails any PR touching a non-test file under `src/` without one\n- [ ] `npm run build`, `npm run typecheck`, `npm test`, `npm run eslint` (`--max-warnings 0`)\n      and `npm run prettier:check` pass, with no `any` in the changed public surface\n\n## Tech notes\n\nToday's `throw` in `registry.ts` propagates through the orchestrator's tool-call\n`Promise.all`, out of the processing loop's `try`, fires `onError`, and reaches the\nWebSocket error handler, which sends a generic \"Failed to process message\" and never sends\nthe stream-end frame. That end-to-end path is why a rejection has to be a returned\n`ToolResponse` instead of a throw.\n\nDeliberately out of scope: the `Unknown tool: <name>` throw stays as-is — the same failure\nclass handled the opposite way, which is an intentional split, but changing it affects\ndirect callers and is tracked as *Return an unknown tool name to the model instead of\nthrowing*. Also out of scope: validating tool *output*; hallucination detection and an\nevaluation harness; per-tool authorization — a schema-valid call for someone else's data is still\nvalid, and authorization is a separate `ToolContext` boundary concern that no issue in this epic\nowns (*Let a consumer decline a tool call through a `beforeToolCall` hook* is a veto seam, not an\nauthorization model); retry, timeout\nand rate limiting around tool execution; and any actual tool implementation, which is\nwritten against this contract later.\n\n---\nOn branch loop-bench/37\nYour branch is up to date with 'origin/loop-bench/37'.\n\nnothing to commit, working tree clean\n8c3318e Merge pull request #83 from re-cinq/lore/implementation-loop/issue-36\n524ad95 Make the hooks comment a JSDoc block so editors Neither command builds first:\n    no test here reads dist/.\n  list: node scripts/lore-list-tests.mjs\n  run: node scripts/lore-run-test.mjs \"{selector}\"\n  coverage_format: lcov","is_error":false}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"dddb36ac-ab14-43e4-a5be-4d1f4b49f13f","uuid":"a94aad01-039c-4dfb-9436-c95ec36db342","timestamp":"2026-11-02T17:56:38.351Z","tool_use_result":{"stdout":"# Validate tool input against the registered inputSchema\n\nIssue: re-cinq/HALEngine#37\nLabels: priority:medium, type:feature, size:M, area:tools, epic:extension-points\n\nPart of #35\n\n## Why\n\n`ToolRegistry.execute` looks a tool up, logs the input's key names, and calls the executor\nwithout ever checking the declared `inputSchema` (`src/orchestration/tools/registry.ts`).\nEvery tool declares a JSON Schema (`src/types/ai.ts`) that is handed verbatim to both\nproviders, but nothing validates what comes back from the model. A test asserts a rejected\n      payload containing an identifying field produces no log line and no returned text\n      containing that field's value\n- [ ] `execute` carries a **one-line** doc comment — `re-lint/max-comment-lines` rejects a\n      `@param`/`@returns`/`@throws` block — and the full contract (rejection `ToolResponse` for input\n      the schema rejects, throw only for an unknown tool name) is stated in\n      `specs/hal-engine-tool-input-validation/spec.md`, which the comment points at\n- [ ] The validator is in `package.json` `dependencies`, not `peerDependencies` or\n      `devDependencies`, and\n      `npm run build && node --input-type=module -e \"import('./dist/index.js')\"` exits `0`\n- [ ] `README.md` states what the registry now does, and the claim is true rather than\n      merely present\n- [ ] `docs/adding-a-tool.md` no longer tells executors to cast defensively and handle\n      missing input; it states that input reaching an executor has already passed the\n      declared schema, that unknown keys may still be present, and that a rejection is\n      returned to the model rather than thrown\n- [ ] `.specify/spec.md` records that a rejection is a tool result (not an exception), that\n      the tool-round bound is what stops a retry cycle from spinning, and that extra\n      properties pass through unmodified\n- [ ] The PR body states the migration note: an executor that used to hand-validate its\n      input now receives only input the schema already accepts, and a tool that relied on\n      seeing malformed input to produce its own error message stops seeing it. The version\n      bump is MINOR\n- [ ] Every test this issue adds cites the statement it validates with an inline\n      `([validated by ...])` link in a `specs/**` or `adrs/**` file, and the statements exist\n      before the tests do. `re-lint/require-spec-link` fails the build on an uncited test, so the\n      spec edits are part of this issue rather than a follow-up\n- [ ] `CHANGELOG.md` carries an entry for this change, written for somebody reading it on npm\n      rather than somebody reading this repo's log. CI runs `scripts/check-changelog.sh` and\n      fails any PR touching a non-test file under `src/` without one\n- [ ] `npm run build`, `npm run typecheck`, `npm test`, `npm run eslint` (`--max-warnings 0`)\n      and `npm run prettier:check` pass, with no `any` in the changed public surface\n\n## Tech notes\n\nToday's `throw` in `registry.ts` propagates through the orchestrator's tool-call\n`Promise.all`, out of the processing loop's `try`, fires `onError`, and reaches the\nWebSocket error handler, which sends a generic \"Failed to process message\" and never sends\nthe stream-end frame. That end-to-end path is why a rejection has to be a returned\n`ToolResponse` instead of a throw.\n\nDeliberately out of scope: the `Unknown tool: <name>` throw stays as-is — the same failure\nclass handled the opposite way, which is an intentional split, but changing it affects\ndirect callers and is tracked as *Return an unknown tool name to the model instead of\nthrowing*. Also out of scope: validating tool *output*; hallucination detection and an\nevaluation harness; per-tool authorization — a schema-valid call for someone else's data is still\nvalid, and authorization is a separate `ToolContext` boundary concern that no issue in this epic\nowns (*Let a consumer decline a tool call through a `beforeToolCall` hook* is a veto seam, not an\nauthorization model); retry, timeout\nand rate limiting around tool execution; and any actual tool implementation, which is\nwritten against this contract later.\n\n---\nOn branch loop-bench/37\nYour branch is up to date with type {ToolDefinition} from '../../types/ai.js';\nimport type {OutgoingMessage} from '../../types/messages.js';\nimport {log} from '../../shared/logger.js';\n\nexport interface ToolResponse {\n  result: string;\n  clientMessages?: OutgoingMessage[];\n  suppressAssistantResponse?: boolean;\n}\n\nexport interface ToolContext {\n  userId: string | number;\n  sessionId?: string;\n  workspaceId?: string | number;\n  authHeaders?: {\n    cookie?: string;\n    authorization?: string;\n    host?: string;\n  };\n}\n\nexport type ToolExecutor = (input: Record<string, unknown>, context?: ToolContext) => Promise<string | ToolResponse>;\n\nexport function normalizeToolResponse(response: string | ToolResponse): ToolResponse {\n  return typeof response === 'string' ? {result: response} : response;\n}\n\nexport type ToolDefinitionSource = ToolDefinition | (() => ToolDefinition);\n\nexport interface RegisteredTool {\n  definition: ToolDefinition;\n  execute: ToolExecutor;\n}\n\ninterface StoredTool {\n  definitionSource: ToolDefinitionSource;\n  execute: ToolExecutor;\n}\n\nexport class ToolRegistry {\n  private readonly tools = new Map<string, StoredTool>();\n\n  private resolveDefinition(source: ToolDefinitionSource): ToolDefinition {\n    return typeof source === 'function' ? source() : source;\n  }\n\n  register(definition: ToolDefinitionSource, execute: ToolExecutor): void {\n    const resolved = this.resolveDefinition(definition);\n    this.tools.set(resolved.name, {definitionSource: definition, execute});\n  }\n\n  get(name: string): RegisteredTool | undefined {\n    const tool = this.tools.get(name);\n    if (!tool) return undefined;\n    return {definition: this.resolveDefinition(tool.definitionSource), execute: tool.execute};\n  }\n\n  getDefinitions(): ToolDefinition[] {\n    return [...this.tools.values()].map(t => this.resolveDefinition(t.definitionSource));\n  }\n\n  getPromptInstructions(): string[] {\n    return this.getDefinitions()\n      .filter(t => t.promptInstructions)\n      .map(t => `${t.name}: ${t.promptInstructions}`);\n  }\n\n  getExamplePrompts(): string[] {\n    return this.getDefinitions().flatMap(t => t.examplePrompts ?? []);\n  }\n\n  has(name: string): boolean {\n    return this.tools.has(name);\n  }\n\n  async execute(name: string, input: Record<string, unknown>, context?: ToolContext): Promise<ToolResponse> {\n    const tool = this.tools.get(name);\n    if (!tool) {\n      log.warn('tool', 'tool not found', {name});\n      throw new Error(`Unknown tool: ${name}`);\n    }\n    log.info('tool', 'executing', {name, inputKeys: Object.keys(input)});\n    const rawResult = await tool.execute(input, context);\n    const response = normalizeToolResponse(rawResult);\n    log.info('tool', 'execution complete', {\n      name,\n      rawType: typeof rawResult,\n      clientMessageCount: response.clientMessages?.length ?? 0,\n      clientMessageTypes: response.clientMessages?.map(m => m.type) ?? [],\n      suppressAssistantResponse: response.suppressAssistantResponse ?? false,\n    });\n    return response;\n  }\n}\n{\n  \"name\": \"@re-cinq/hal-engine\",\n  \"version\": \"0.3.0\",\n type {ToolDefinition} from '../../types/ai.js';\nimport type {OutgoingMessage} from '../../types/messages.js';\nimport {log} from '../../shared/logger.js';\n\nexport interface ToolResponse {\n  result: string;\n  clientMessages?: OutgoingMessage[];\n  suppressAssistantResponse?: boolean;\n}\n\nexport interface ToolContext {\n  userId: string | number;\n  sessionId?: string;\n  workspaceId?: string | number;\n  authHeaders?: {\n    cookie?: string;\n    authorization?: string;\n    host?: string;\n  };\n}\n\nexport type ToolExecutor = (input: Record<string, unknown>, context?: ToolContext) => Promise<string | ToolResponse>;\n\nexport function normalizeToolResponse(response: string | ToolResponse): ToolResponse {\n  return typeof response === 'string' ? {result: response} : response;\n}\n\nexport type ToolDefinitionSource = ToolDefinition | (() => ToolDefinition);\n\nexport interface RegisteredTool {\n  definition: ToolDefinition;\n  execute: ToolExecutor;\n}\n\ninterface StoredTool {\n  definitionSource: ToolDefinitionSource;\n  execute: ToolExecutor;\n}\n\nexport class ToolRegistry {\n  private readonly tools = new Map<string, StoredTool>();\n\n  private resolveDefinition(source: ToolDefinitionSource): ToolDefinition {\n    return typeof source === 'function' ? source() : source;\n  }\n\n  register(definition: ToolDefinitionSource, execute: ToolExecutor): void {\n    const resolved = this.resolveDefinition(definition);\n    this.tools.set(resolved.name, {definitionSource: definition, execute});\n  }\n\n  get(name: string): RegisteredTool | undefined {\n    const tool = this.tools.get(name);\n    if (!tool) return undefined;\n    return {definition: this.resolveDefinition(tool.definitionSource), execute: tool.execute};\n  }\n\n  getDefinitions(): ToolDefinition[] {\n    return [...this.tools.values()].map(t => this.resolveDefinition(t.definitionSource));\n  }\n\n  getPromptInstructions(): string[] {\n    return this.getDefinitions()\n      .filter(t => t.promptInstructions)\n      .map(t => `${t.name}: ${t.promptInstructions}`);\n  }\n\n  getExamplePrompts(): string[] {\n    return this.getDefinitions().flatMap(t => t.examplePrompts ?? []);\n  }\n\n  has(name: string): boolean {\n    return this.tools.has(name);\n  }\n\n  async execute(name: string, input: Record<string, unknown>, context?: ToolContext): Promise<ToolResponse> {\n    const tool = this.tools.get(name);\n    if (!tool) {\n      log.warn('tool', 'tool not found', {name});\n      throw new Error(`Unknown tool: ${name}`);\n    }\n    log.info('tool', 'executing', {name, inputKeys: Object.keys(input)});\n    const rawResult = await tool.execute(input, context);\n    const response = normalizeToolResponse(rawResult);\n    log.info('tool', 'execution complete', {\n      name,\n      rawType: typeof rawResult,\n      clientMessageCount: response.clientMessages?.length ?? 0,\n      clientMessageTypes: response.clientMessages?.map(m => m.type) ?? [],\n      suppressAssistantResponse: response.suppressAssistantResponse ?? false,\n    });\n    return response;\n  }\n}\n{\n  \"name\": \"@re-cinq/hal-engine\",\n  \"version\": \"0.3.0\",\n {"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"toolu_01DvQqXwsH6gq6AsmVJPtjxN","type":"tool_result","content":"# Tool Responses\n\n| Field  | Value |\n| ------ | ----- |\n| Issue  | n/a   |\n| Status | Draft |\n\nBy default, a tool executor returns a plain string that feeds back into the AI conversation. The client never sees the result -- it only sees the tool invocation (name and input parameters). This is fine for most tools, but sometimes a tool knows exactly what to show the user and does not need the AI to rephrase it.\n\nThe `ToolResponse` interface gives tools two additional capabilities:\n\n1. **Client messages** -- send one or more WebSocket messages directly to the client\n2. **Suppress the AI response** -- keep the model's reply in conversation context but hide it from the client\n\nThese are opt-in. Tools that return a plain string continue to work exactly as before.\n\n## The ToolResponse Interface\n\n<!-- doc-block: src/orchestration/tools/registry.ts#ToolResponse -->\n```typescript\ninterface ToolResponse {\n  result: string;\n  clientMessages?: OutgoingMessage[];\n  suppressAssistantResponse?: boolean;\n}\n```\n\n| Field | Purpose |\n|-------|---------|\n| `result` | The string the AI model sees -- identical to what a plain-string executor returns |\n| `clientMessages` | Optional array of `OutgoingMessage` values sent directly to the client via WebSocket |\n| `suppressAssistantResponse` | When `true`, the AI's next text response still accumulates in `session.entries` for context continuity, but is not sent to the client |\n\nA `ToolExecutor` can return either a plain `string` or a `ToolResponse`:\n\n<!-- doc-block: src/orchestration/tools/registry.ts#ToolExecutor -->\n```typescript\ntype ToolExecutor = (input: Record<string, unknown>, context?: ToolContext) => Promise<string | ToolResponse>;\n```\n\n`context` carries the session the call belongs to -- `userId`, `sessionId`, `workspaceId`, and the `authHeaders` forwarded from the WebSocket upgrade, so a tool can proxy the caller's credentials upstream.\n\nThe registry normalizes both forms internally via `normalizeToolResponse()`, so downstream code always sees a `ToolResponse`.\n\n## When to Use This\n\n**Client messages** make sense when the tool result is self-explanatory and the AI does not need to interpret it for the user. A unit conversion, a status update, a formatted data table -- these are cases where the tool knows the best presentation and the AI would just parrot the same information.\n\n**Suppression** pairs naturally with client messages. If the tool already sent the answer to the client, the AI's follow-up (\"The conversion result is 160.93 km\") adds no value. Suppression hides that echo while keeping it in the conversation so the AI remembers the context for follow-up questions.\n\nUse plain string returns (the default) when the AI needs to interpret or summarize the tool result for the user. Database lookups and search results are good examples -- the raw data benefits from the AI explaining it in context.\n\n## Data Flow\n\n```\nPlain string:    executor returns \"data\"\n                   -> normalizeToolResponse wraps to {result: \"data\"}\n                   -> orchestrator feeds result to AI\n                   -> AI responds, client sees the response\n\nWith client messages:\n                 executor returns {result, clientMessages, suppressAssistantResponse}\n                   -> orchestrator extracts .result for AI (unchanged)\n                   -> orchestrator yields tool_result chunk carrying clientMessages\n                   -> if suppress, orchestrator yields suppress_output chunk\n                   -> messageHandler sends clientMessages to client\n                   -> messageHandler blanks pre-tool assistant entries (entry_upsert with empty content)\n                   -> messageHandler skips WebSocket sends for suppressed entries\n                   -> messageHandler sends entry_skip for each new suppressed entry\n                   -> suppressed entries still accumulate in session.entries\n                   -> client adjusts incoming indices using tracked skip list\n```\n\nThe key insight: suppressed entries are invisible to the client but visible to the AI. The conversation context stays intact, so the model can reference previous tool results even when the user did not see the AI's commentary on them.\n\n## Example: Unit Converter\n\nThe `convert_units` tool demonstrates all three features: client messages, suppression, and index assignment. A conversion like \"100 miles to kilometers\" has a definitive answer -- the AI does not need to elaborate.\n\n<!-- doc-block: none -- a simplified tool, marked simplified in its own first line -->\n```typescript\n// src/orchestration/tools/unitConverterTool.ts (simplified)\n\nexport const executeUnitConverter: ToolExecutor = async (input) => {\n  const value = input.value as number;\n  const fromUnit = (input.from_unit as string) || '';\n  const toUnit = (input.to_unit as string) || '';\n\n  const result = convert(value, fromUnit, toUnit);\n\n  if (!result) {\n    // Error case: return plain string so AI can explain the problem\n    return JSON.stringify({error: `Cannot convert from \"${fromUnit}\" to \"${toUnit}\".`});\n  }\n\n  return {\n    result: JSON.stringify({value, fromUnit, toUnit, converted: result.converted, formatted: result.formatted}),\n    clientMessages: [\n      {\n        type: 'entry_upsert',\n        index: 0,\n        entry: {role: 'assistant', content: result.formatted, timestamp: new Date().toISOString(), isStreaming: false},\n      },\n    ],\n    suppressAssistantResponse: true,\n  };\n};\n```\n\nNotice three things:\n\n1. On success, the executor returns a `clientMessages` array with an `entry_upsert` containing the formatted result. The client receives this as an assistant entry. The `index` is set to `0` -- the orchestrator replaces it with the correct session index (see \"Index assignment\" below).\n\n2. `suppressAssistantResponse: true` hides the AI's follow-up. The AI still receives the result string (so it has context for follow-ups), but the client does not see the AI echoing \"100 miles equals 160.93 km.\"\n\n3. On error, the executor returns a plain string. This lets the AI explain what went wrong in its own words -- \"I could not convert miles to knots because they measure different things.\"\n\n## Adding Client Messages\n\nIf the tool wants to send a custom message to the client, use `clientMessages`. Any valid `OutgoingMessage` type works -- `entry_upsert`, `entry_delta`, `entry_commit`, or `error`.\n\n<!-- doc-block: none -- a worked executor showing one return shape, not code this package exports -->\n```typescript\nexport const executeMyTool: ToolExecutor = async (input) => {\n  const data = await fetchSomething(input);\n\n  return {\n    result: JSON.stringify(data),\n    clientMessages: [\n      {type: 'entry_upsert', index: 0, entry: {\n        role: 'assistant' as const,\n        content: formatData(data),\n        timestamp: new Date().toISOString(),\n        isStreaming: false,\n      }},\n    ],\n  };\n};\n```\n\nClient messages are forwarded to the WebSocket after index assignment. The client processes them through its existing message dispatch pipeline -- no client-side changes needed.\n\n### Index assignment for entry_upsert\n\nTools do not have access to the session, so they cannot know the correct entry index. The orchestrator handles this automatically: any `entry_upsert` message in `clientMessages` gets its `index` replaced with the real value by appending the entry to the session. The tool can set `index` to any value -- it will be overwritten.\n\nThis means the entry also becomes part of the session's `entries` array, maintaining context continuity for the AI model.\n\n## How It Works Under the Hood\n\n### Orchestrator\n\nAfter executing tool calls, `chatOrchestrator.ts` checks each `ToolResponse` for client messages and suppression flags:\n\n<!-- doc-block: none -- the orchestrator fragment that normalises responses, quoted out of its function -->\n```typescript\nconst responses = await Promise.all(\n  pendingToolCalls.map(async (tc) => {\n    const response = await toolRegistry.execute(tc.name, tc.input);\n    return {tc, response};\n  })\n);\n\n// Collect client messages from all tools\nconst clientMessages = responses.flatMap(({response}) => response.clientMessages ?? []);\n\n// Yield them as a chunk for the message handler\nif (clientMessages.length > 0) {\n  yield {type: 'tool_result', clientMessages};\n}\n\n// Signal suppression if any tool requested it\nif (responses.some(({response}) => response.suppressAssistantResponse)) {\n  yield {type: 'suppress_output'};\n}\n```\n\n### Message Handler\n\nTwo new chunk types flow through `processChunk`:\n\n- `tool_result` -- each client message in the array is serialized and sent directly to the WebSocket\n- `suppress_output` -- sets `state.suppressOutput = true` on the stream state\n\nWhen suppression is active, `streamSegment` and `commitAndClear` still call `appendEntry`, `appendDelta`, and `commitEntry` (so the session accumulates entries for context), but skip `sendUpsert`, `sendDelta`, and `sendCommit` (so the client sees nothing).\n\nWhen a new entry is created under suppression, the server sends an `entry_skip` message with the server-side index. This tells the client \"an entry exists here on the server, but you will never receive it.\" The client tracks these skipped indices and adjusts all subsequent incoming indices via the `adjustIndex` pure function. Without this, server indices would diverge from the client's entry array, causing deltas and commits to target the wrong positions.\n\nIf the AI streamed text *before* the tool call (which happens when the model explains what it is about to do), that text was already sent to the client as an assistant entry. When suppression activates, the server retroactively blanks these entries by sending `entry_upsert` with empty content. The client already skips rendering empty assistant entries, so the pre-tool text disappears cleanly.\n\n**Suppression is sticky for the entire stream.** Once a tool sets `suppressAssistantResponse: true` in any round, all subsequent AI responses in that stream are suppressed -- even if later tool rounds do not set the flag. This is intentional: suppression is a per-message property, not per-round. If the first tool already sent the definitive answer to the client, the AI's commentary in all following rounds is equally redundant. A new user message starts a fresh stream with `suppressOutput: false`.\n\n### stream_end and the spinner\n\nThe server sends a `stream_end` message after every completed stream (see [websocket-protocol.md](../hal-engine-websocket-protocol/spec.md)). This is the client's signal to clear loading indicators.\n\nWithout `stream_end`, suppression would cause the tool spinner to hang forever. When the assistant response is suppressed, no `entry_upsert` arrives after the tool entry, so the tool stays as the last entry in the array. The client tracks an `isProcessing` state: `true` when the user sends a message, `false` when `stream_end` arrives. The spinner only shows when the tool is both last in the list *and* the stream is still processing.\n\n### MessageChunk union\n\nThe `MessageChunk` type gained two variants:\n\n<!-- doc-block: src/types/ai.ts#MessageChunk -->\n```typescript\ntype MessageChunk =\n  | {type: 'text'; text: string}\n```\n\nThese two variants are internal plumbing -- they never leave the server. The client only sees standard `OutgoingMessage` types over the WebSocket.\n\n## File Reference\n\n| File | What changed |\n|------|-------------|\n| `src/orchestration/tools/registry.ts` | `ToolResponse` interface, widened `ToolExecutor`, `normalizeToolResponse()` |\n| `src/types/ai.ts` | `tool_result` and `suppress_output` variants on `MessageChunk` |\n| `src/orchestration/chatOrchestrator.ts` | `executeToolCalls` returns `ToolExecutionResult`, yields new chunks |\n| `src/transport/ws/messageHandler.ts` | `suppressOutput` on stream state, `processToolResultChunk`, guarded sends, `entry_skip`, blanking |\n----\nspecs/hal-engine-config-hooks/spec.md:12:- A hook passed through `orchestrator.hooks` is forwarded to the orchestrator and fires ([validated by: forwards an orchestrator hook, so one passed through the config actually fires](../../src/config.test.ts#L17)).\nspecs/hal-engine-config-hooks/spec.md:13:- An engine built with no `orchestrator` key at all still processes a message, so the field is optional in fact and not only in the type ([validated by: processes a message when the config declares no orchestrator key at all](../../src/config.test.ts#L93)).\nspecs/hal-engine-config-hooks/spec.md:14:- A `beforeModelResponse` hook installed through config is handed the base system prompt built from `prompt`, proving the hook reaches the orchestrator and is wired into the prompt pipeline rather than only being stored on the config ([validated by: hands a config-installed beforeModelResponse hook the built base system prompt, not merely storing it](../../src/config.test.ts#L106)).\nspecs/hal-engine-config-hooks/spec.md:15:- The value the hook returns then replaces the system prompt the provider receives; this is exercised at the orchestrator layer because `HalEngineConfig.provider` takes a `ProviderConfig` and offers no seam to inject a recording provider through config ([validated by: replaces system prompt with returned value](../../src/orchestration/chatOrchestrator.test.ts#L194)).\nspecs/hal-engine-config-hooks/spec.md:16:- For a session that completes without error the hooks fire in the order `beforeSession` → `beforeUserInput` → `afterUserInput` → `beforeModelResponse` → `afterModelResponse` → `afterSession` ([validated by: fires the lifecycle hooks in documented order for a session that completes without error](../../src/config.test.ts#L130)).\nspecs/hal-engine-config-hooks/spec.md:20:- The engine's `start()` method rejects the returned promise when it cannot bind the configured port, rather than emitting an unhandled error event that would take the process down ([validated by: rejects instead of taking the process down with an unhandled error event](../../src/config.test.ts#L172)).\nspecs/hal-engine-chat-routes/spec.md:17:- The chat is looked up second. An id with no chat is `404 Chat not found` ([validated by: answers 404 for a chat that does not exist](../../src/transport/routes/chats.test.ts#L77)).\nspecs/hal-engine-chat-routes/spec.md:18:- Ownership is checked third. A caller who is not the recorded owner gets `403 Forbidden` and no part of the chat ([validated by: refuses a different user with 403 and no chat content](../../src/transport/routes/chats.test.ts#L68)).\nspecs/hal-engine-chat-routes/spec.md:19:- A caller who is not the owner still sees `404` for an id that does not exist, so ownership never becomes an oracle for which ids are real ([validated by: answers 404 before 403, so a wrong owner cannot probe which ids exist](../../src/transport/routes/chats.test.ts#L85)).\nspecs/hal-engine-chat-routes/spec.md:20:- Owner ids are compared strictly, so numeric `1` and string `\"1\"` are different users rather than the same one ([validated by: compares owner ids strictly, so numeric 1 and string \"1\" are different users](../../src/transport/routes/chats.test.ts#L103)).\n----\n.specify/spec.md:5:HAL Engine is a generic, production-ready AI chat server framework designed to abstract away the complexity of building AI-powered chat backends. It provides a pluggable architecture for AI providers, tools, authentication, session management, and transport, allowing developers to focus on domain-specific logic rather than infrastructure plumbing.\n.specify/spec.md:7:The system handles core patterns including streaming responses, tool execution loops, WebSocket connections, session persistence, and message orchestration. It is structured as a layered application with clear separation of concerns from core types through infrastructure and transport.\n.specify/spec.md:28:- Dynamic tool registration via `ToolRegistry`\n.specify/spec.md:29:- JSON Schema tool definitions, declared per tool and forwarded to the model; the schema describes the input the model should produce and is NOT enforced before the executor runs\n.specify/spec.md:30:- Parallel tool execution within configurable tool loops\n.specify/spec.md:32:- Context-aware tool execution: the executor receives the caller's identity (`userId`, `sessionId`, `workspaceId`) and the auth headers the session carries, forwarded from the WebSocket upgrade or the HTTP chat request, not the conversation history\n.specify/spec.md:33:- Configurable maximum tool loop iterations (default: 5)\n.specify/spec.md:88:- `EntrySkipMessage`: Skip tool/response generation\n.specify/spec.md:106:- `AssistantEntry`: AI-generated response with tool calls\n.specify/spec.md:118:- `ToolCallContent`: Structured tool call representation\n.specify/spec.md:119:- `ToolResultContent`: Structured tool result representation\n.specify/spec.md:147:- Sets rate limits and tool execution constraints\n.specify/spec.md:154:- Registers domain-specific tools via `ToolRegistry`\n.specify/spec.md:165:- Observes tool execution through entry protocol\n.specify/spec.md:171:- Generates text responses and tool calls\n.specify/spec.md:174:- Validates tool calls against schemas\n.specify/spec.md:183:- Maximum tool loop iterations enforce termination (prevents infinite loops)\n.specify/spec.md:191:- Tool results must follow corresponding tool calls\n.specify/spec.md:192:- Thinking entries (when present) appear before tool calls or text\n.specify/spec.md:201:- User context is available to tools and infrastructure components\n.specify/spec.md:224:- Tool results are validated against original tool definition schema\n.specify/spec.md:245:4. **Error Rate**: Failed messages, tool execution errors, provider failures\n.specify/spec.md:249:1. **Tool Success Rate**: Percentage of tool calls that execute without error\n.specify/spec.md:255:1. **Time to Integration**: Hours to implement custom provider or tool\n.specify/spec.md:268:2. **Tool Execution Idempotency**: Safe to retry failed tool calls\n.specify/spec.md:281:- **Layer 4 (Orchestration)**: Chat orchestrator, tool registry, conversation context, entry management\n.specify/spec.md:298:- `tools`: Custom tool registry (defaults to empty)\n.specify/spec.md:302:- `orchestrator`: Max tool rounds, context config, lifecycle hooks\n----\nsrc/orchestration/tools:\nindex.ts\nregistry.ts\n\nsrc/types:\nai.ts\nauth.ts\nindex.ts\nmessages.ts\npromptStore.ts\nsession.ts\nsessionStore.ts\nusageStore.ts\nimport type {OutgoingMessage} from './messages.js';\n\nexport interface TextMessage {\n  role: 'user' | 'assistant';\n  content: string;\n}\n\nexport interface ToolCallContent {\n  type: 'tool_use';\n  toolUseId: string;\n  name: string;\n  input: Record<string, unknown>;\n}\n\nexport interface ToolResultContent {\n  type: 'tool_result';\n  toolUseId: string;\n  content: string;\n}\n\nexport interface ToolUseMessage {\n  role: 'assistant';\n  content: ToolCallContent[];\n}\n\nexport interface ToolResultMessage {\n  role: 'user';\n  content: ToolResultContent[];\n}\n\nexport type Message = TextMessage | ToolUseMessage | ToolResultMessage;\n\nexport interface ToolDefinition {\n  name: string;\n  description: string;\n  inputSchema: Record<string, unknown>;\n  promptInstructions?: string;\n  examplePrompts?: string[];\n}\n\nexport interface ToolCall {\n  id: string;\n  name: string;\n  input: Record<string, unknown>;\n}\n\nexport type MessageChunk =\n  | {type: 'text'; text: string}\n  | {type: 'tool_use'; toolCall: ToolCall}\n  | {type: 'tool_result'; clientMessages: OutgoingMessage[]}\n  | {type: 'suppress_output'}\n  | {type: 'stop'; stopReason: string; usage?: UsageMetadata};\n\nexport interface SendMessageParams {\n  messages: Message[];\n  systemPrompt: string;\n  tools?: ToolDefinition[];\n  maxTokens?: number;\n}\n\nexport interface StructuredOutputParams<_T> {\n  messages: Message[];\n  systemPrompt: string;\n  responseSchema: ResponseSchema;\n  maxTokens?: number;\n}\n\nexport interface ResponseSchema {\n  type: 'object' | 'array' | 'string' | 'number' | 'boolean';\n  properties?: Record<string, ResponseSchema>;\n  items?: ResponseSchema;\n  required?: string[];\n  description?: string;\n  enum?: string[];\n}\n\nexport interface UsageMetadata {\n  inputTokens: number;\n  outputTokens: number;\n  totalTokens: number;\nfull-config.ts\nminimal.ts\nregister-tool.ts\nserver.ts\nweather-tool.ts\nwith-tools.ts\n// The worked tool from docs/adding-a-tool.md, as a file the compiler checks.\n\n// #region weather-tool\nimport type {ToolDefinition, ToolExecutor} from '../src/index.js';\n\nexport const weatherTool: ToolDefinition = {\n  name: 'get_weather',\n  description:\n    'Get the current weather for a location.\\n\\n' +\n    'Use this tool when the user:\\n' +\n    '* Asks about weather or temperature\\n' +\n    '* Wants to know conditions at a specific place\\n' +\n    '* Needs a forecast for planning\\n\\n' +\n    'Returns temperature, conditions, humidity, and wind speed.',\n  promptInstructions:\n    'Use this when users ask about weather at a specific location. ' +\n    'Call this for questions like \"What is the weather in Berlin?\" or \"Is it raining in Tokyo?\"',\n  examplePrompts: ['What is the weather in Berlin?', 'Is it raining in Tokyo?'],\n  inputSchema: {\n    type: 'object',\n    properties: {\n      location: {\n        type: 'string',\n        description: 'The city name or location to look up.',\n      },\n      units: {\n        type: 'string',\n        enum: ['celsius', 'fahrenheit'],\n        description: 'Temperature units. Defaults to celsius.',\n      },\n    },\n    required: ['location'],\n  },\n};\n\nexport const executeWeather: ToolExecutor = async input => {\n  const location = (input.location as string) || 'Unknown';\n  const units = (input.units as string) || 'celsius';\n\n  // Replace with a real API call. `input` is untrusted: inputSchema shapes what the model sends, it does not enforce it.\n  const result = {\n    location,\n    temperature: 18,\n    units,\n    conditions: 'Partly cloudy',\n    humidity: 65,\n    windSpeed: 12,\n    windUnit: 'km/h',\n  };\n\n  return JSON.stringify(result);\n};\n// #endregion\n8c3318e Merge pull request #83 from re-cinq/lore/implementation-loop/issue-36\n524ad95 Make the hooks comment a JSDoc block so editors show it\n130b266 State the consequence of last-round usage and chain the optional headers\n* loop-bench/37\n  main\n  remotes/origin/HEAD -> origin/main\n  remotes/origin/loop-bench/37\n  remotes/origin/main","is_error":false}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"dddb36ac-ab14-43e4-a5be-4d1f4b49f13f","uuid":"87631501-9708-4b82-b93f-7e63f6837f94","timestamp":"2026-11-02T17:56:45.808Z","tool_use_result":{"stdout":"# Tool Responses\n\n| Field  | Value |\n| ------ | ----- |\n| Issue  | n/a   |\n| Status | Draft |\n\nBy default, a tool executor returns a plain string that feeds back into the AI conversation. The client never sees the result -- it only sees the tool invocation (name and input parameters). This is fine for most tools, but sometimes a tool knows exactly what to show the user and does not need the AI to rephrase it.\n\nThe `ToolResponse` interface gives tools two additional capabilities:\n\n1. **Client messages** -- send one or more WebSocket messages directly to the client\n2. **Suppress the AI response** -- keep the model's reply in conversation context but hide it from the client\n\nThese are opt-in. Tools that return a plain string continue to work exactly as before.\n\n## The ToolResponse Interface\n\n<!-- doc-block: src/orchestration/tools/registry.ts#ToolResponse -->\n```typescript\ninterface ToolResponse {\n  result: string;\n  clientMessages?: OutgoingMessage[];\n  suppressAssistantResponse?: boolean;\n}\n```\n\n| Field | Purpose |\n|-------|---------|\n| `result` | The string the AI model sees -- identical to what a plain-string executor returns |\n| `clientMessages` | Optional array of `OutgoingMessage` values sent directly to the client via WebSocket |\n| `suppressAssistantResponse` | When `true`, the AI's next text response still accumulates in `session.entries` for context continuity, but is not sent to the client |\n\nA `ToolExecutor` can return either a plain `string` or a `ToolResponse`:\n\n<!-- doc-block: src/orchestration/tools/registry.ts#ToolExecutor -->\n```typescript\ntype ToolExecutor = (input: Record<string, unknown>, context?: ToolContext) => Promise<string | ToolResponse>;\n```\n\n`context` carries the session the call belongs to -- `userId`, `sessionId`, `workspaceId`, and the `authHeaders` forwarded from the WebSocket upgrade, so a tool can proxy the caller's credentials upstream.\n\nThe registry normalizes both forms internally via `normalizeToolResponse()`, so downstream code always sees a `ToolResponse`.\n\n## When to Use This\n\n**Client messages** make sense when the tool result is self-explanatory and the AI does not need to interpret it for the user. A unit conversion, a status update, a formatted data table -- these are cases where the tool knows the best presentation and the AI would just parrot the same information.\n\n**Suppression** pairs naturally with client messages. If the tool already sent the answer to the client, the AI's follow-up (\"The conversion result is 160.93 km\") adds no value. Suppression hides that echo while keeping it in the conversation so the AI remembers the context for follow-up questions.\n\nUse plain string returns (the default) when the AI needs to interpret or summarize the tool result for the user. Database lookups and search results are good examples -- the raw data benefits from the AI explaining it in context.\n\n## Data Flow\n\n```\nPlain string:    executor returns \"data\"\n                   -> normalizeToolResponse wraps to {result: \"data\"}\n                   -> orchestrator feeds result to AI\n                   -> AI responds, client sees the response\n\nWith client messages:\n                 executor returns {result, clientMessages, suppressAssistantResponse}\n                   -> orchestrator extracts .result for AI (unchanged)\n                   -> orchestrator yields tool_result chunk carrying clientMessages\n                   -> if suppress, orchestrator yields suppress_output chunk\n                   -> messageHandler sends clientMessages to client\n                   -> messageHandler blanks pre-tool assistant entries (entry_upsert with empty content)\n                   -> messageHandler skips WebSocket sends for suppressed entries\n                   -> messageHandler sends entry_skip for each new suppressed entry\n                   -> suppressed entries still accumulate in session.entries\n                   -> client adjusts incoming indices using tracked skip list\n```\n\nThe key insight: suppressed entries are invisible to the client but visible to the AI. The conversation context stays intact, so the model can reference previous tool results even when the user did not see the AI's commentary on them.\n\n## Example: Unit Converter\n\nThe `convert_units` tool demonstrates all three features: client messages, suppression, and index assignment. A conversion like \"100 miles to kilometers\" has a definitive answer -- the AI does not need to elaborate.\n\n<!-- doc-block: none -- a simplified tool, marked simplified in its own first line -->\n```typescript\n// src/orchestration/tools/unitConverterTool.ts (simplified)\n\nexport const executeUnitConverter: ToolExecutor = async (input) => {\n  const value = input.value as number;\n  const fromUnit = (input.from_unit as string) || '';\n  const toUnit = (input.to_unit as string) || '';\n\n  const result = convert(value, fromUnit, toUnit);\n\n  if (!result) {\n    // Error case: return plain string so AI can explain the problem\n    return JSON.stringify({error: `Cannot convert from \"${fromUnit}\" to \"${toUnit}\".`});\n  }\n\n  return {\n    result: JSON.stringify({value, fromUnit, toUnit, converted: result.converted, formatted: result.formatted}),\n    clientMessages: [\n      {\n        type: 'entry_upsert',\n        index: 0,\n        entry: {role: 'assistant', content: result.formatted, timestamp: new Date().toISOString(), isStreaming: false},\n      },\n    ],\n    suppressAssistantResponse: true,\n  };\n};\n```\n\nNotice three things:\n\n1. On success, the executor returns a `clientMessages` array with an `entry_upsert` containing the formatted result. The client receives this as an assistant entry. The `index` is set to `0` -- the orchestrator replaces it with the correct session index (see \"Index assignment\" below).\n\n2. `suppressAssistantResponse: true` hides the AI's follow-up. The AI still receives the result string (so it has context for follow-ups), but the client does not see the AI echoing \"100 miles equals 160.93 km.\"\n\n3. On error, the executor returns a plain string. This lets the AI explain what went wrong in its own words -- \"I could not convert miles to knots because they measure different things.\"\n\n## Adding Client Messages\n\nIf the tool wants to send a custom message to the client, use `clientMessages`. Any valid `OutgoingMessage` type works -- `entry_upsert`, `entry_delta`, `entry_commit`, or `error`.\n\n<!-- doc-block: none -- a worked executor showing one return shape, not code this package exports -->\n```typescript\nexport const executeMyTool: ToolExecutor = async (input) => {\n  const data = await fetchSomething(input);\n\n  return {\n    result: JSON.stringify(data),\n    clientMessages: [\n      {type: 'entry_upsert', index: 0, entry: {\n        role: 'assistant' as const,\n        content: formatData(data),\n        timestamp: new Date().toISOString(),\n        isStreaming: false,\n      }},\n    ],\n  };\n};\n```\n\nClient messages are forwarded to the WebSocket after index assignment. The client processes them through its existing message dispatch pipeline -- no client-side changes needed.\n\n### Index assignment for entry_upsert\n\nTools do not have access to the session, so they cannot know the correct entry index. The orchestrator handles this automatically: any `entry_upsert` message in `clientMessages` gets its `index` replaced with the real value by appending the entry to the session. The tool can set `index` to any value -- it will be overwritten.\n\nThis means the entry also becomes part of the session's `entries` array, maintaining context continuity for the AI model.\n\n## How It Works Under the Hood\n\n### Orchestrator\n\nAfter executing tool calls, `chatOrchestrator.ts` checks each `ToolResponse` for client messages and suppression flags:\n\n<!-- doc-block: none -- the orchestrator fragment that normalises responses, quoted out of its function -->\n```typescript\nconst responses = await Promise.all(\n  pendingToolCalls.map(async (tc) => {\n    const response = await toolRegistry.execute(tc.name, tc.input);\n    return {tc, response};\n  })\n);\n\n// Collect client messages from all tools\nconst clientMessages = responses.flatMap(({response}) => response.clientMessages ?? []);\n\n// Yield them as a chunk for the message handler\nif (clientMessages.length > 0) {\n  yield {type: 'tool_result', clientMessages};\n}\n\n// Signal suppression if any tool requested it\nif (responses.some(({response}) => response.suppressAssistantResponse)) {\n  yield {type: 'suppress_output'};\n}\n```\n\n### Message Handler\n\nTwo new chunk types flow through `processChunk`:\n\n- `tool_result` -- each client message in the array is serialized and sent directly to the WebSocket\n- `suppress_output` -- sets `state.suppressOutput = true` on the stream state\n\nWhen suppression is active, `streamSegment` and `commitAndClear` still call `appendEntry`, `appendDelta`, and `commitEntry` (so the session accumulates entries for context), but skip `sendUpsert`, `sendDelta`, and `sendCommit` (so the client sees nothing).\n\nWhen a new entry is created under suppression, the server sends an `entry_skip` message with the server-side index. This tells the client \"an entry exists here on the server, but you will never receive it.\" The client tracks these skipped indices and adjusts all subsequent incoming indices via the `adjustIndex` pure function. Without this, server indices would diverge from the client's entry array, causing deltas and commits to target the wrong positions.\n\nIf the AI streamed text *before* the tool call (which happens when the model explains what it is about to do), that text was already sent to the client as an assistant entry. When suppression activates, the server retroactively blanks these entries by sending `entry_upsert` with empty content. The client already skips rendering empty assistant entries, so the pre-tool text disappears cleanly.\n\n**Suppression is sticky for the entire stream.** Once a tool sets `suppressAssistantResponse: true` in any round, all subsequent AI responses in that stream are suppressed -- even if later tool rounds do not set the flag. This is intentional: suppression is a per-message property, not per-round. If the first tool already sent the definitive answer to the client, the AI's commentary in all following rounds is equally redundant. A new user message starts a fresh stream with `suppressOutput: false`.\n\n### stream_end and the spinner\n\nThe server sends a `stream_end` message after every completed stream (see [websocket-protocol.md](../hal-engine-websocket-protocol/spec.md)). This is the client's signal to clear loading indicators.\n\nWithout `stream_end`, suppression would cause the tool spinner to hang forever. When the assistant response is suppressed, no `entry_upsert` arrives after the tool entry, so the tool stays as the last entry in the array. The client tracks an `isProcessing` state: `true` when the user sends a message, `false` when `stream_end` arrives. The spinner only shows when the tool is both last in the list *and* the stream is still processing.\n\n### MessageChunk union\n\nThe `MessageChunk` type gained two variants:\n\n<!-- doc-block: src/types/ai.ts#MessageChunk -->\n```typescript\ntype MessageChunk =\n  | {type: 'text'; text: string}\n```\n\nThese two variants are internal plumbing -- they never leave the server. The client only sees standard `OutgoingMessage` types over the WebSocket.\n\n## File Reference\n\n| File | What changed |\n|------|-------------|\n| `src/orchestration/tools/registry.ts` | `ToolResponse` interface, widened `ToolExecutor`, `normalizeToolResponse()` |\n| `src/types/ai.ts` | `tool_result` and `suppress_output` variants on `MessageChunk` |\n| `src/orchestration/chatOrchestrator.ts` | `executeToolCalls` returns `ToolExecutionResult`, yields new chunks |\n| `src/transport/ws/messageHandler.ts` | `suppressOutput` on stream state, `processToolResultChunk`, guarded sends, `entry_skip`, blanking |\n----\nspecs/hal-engine-config-hooks/spec.md:12:- A hook passed through `orchestrator.hooks` is forwarded to the orchestrator and fires ([validated by: forwards an orchestrator hook, so one passed through the config actually fires](../../src/config.test.ts#L17)).\nspecs/hal-engine-config-hooks/spec.md:13:- An engine built with no `orchestrator` key at all still processes a message, so the field is optional in fact and not only in the type ([validated by: processes a message when the config declares no orchestrator key at all](../../src/config.test.ts#L93)).\nspecs/hal-engine-config-hooks/spec.md:14:- A `beforeModelResponse` hook installed through config is handed the base system prompt built from `prompt`, proving the hook reaches the orchestrator and is wired into the prompt pipeline rather than only being stored on the config ([validated by: hands a config-installed beforeModelResponse hook the built base system prompt, not merely storing it](../../src/config.test.ts#L106)).\nspecs/hal-engine-config-hooks/spec.md:15:- The value the hook returns then replaces the system prompt the provider receives; this is exercised at the orchestrator layer because `HalEngineConfig.provider` takes a `ProviderConfig` and offers no seam to inject a recording provider through config ([validated by: replaces system prompt with returned value](../../src/orchestration/chatOrchestrator.test.ts#L194)).\nspecs/hal-engine-config-hooks/spec.md:16:- For a session that completes without error the hooks fire in the order `beforeSession` → `beforeUserInput` → `afterUserInput` → `beforeModelResponse` → `afterModelResponse` → `afterSession` ([validated by: fires the lifecycle hooks in documented order for a session that completes without error](../../src/config.test.ts#L130)).\nspecs/hal-engine-config-hooks/spec.md:20:- The engine's `start()` method rejects the returned promise when it cannot bind the configured port, rather than emitting an unhandled error event that would take the process down ([validated by: rejects instead of taking the process down with an unhandled error event](../../src/config.test.ts#L172)).\nspecs/hal-engine-chat-routes/spec.md:17:- The chat is looked up second. An id with no chat is `404 Chat not found` ([validated by: answers 404 for a chat that does not exist](../../src/transport/routes/chats.test.ts#L77)).\nspecs/hal-engine-chat-routes/spec.md:18:- Ownership is checked third. A caller who is not the recorded owner gets `403 Forbidden` and no part of the chat ([validated by: refuses a different user with 403 and no chat content](../../src/transport/routes/chats.test.ts#L68)).\nspecs/hal-engine-chat-routes/spec.md:19:- A caller who is not the owner still sees `404` for an id that does not exist, so ownership never becomes an oracle for which ids are real ([validated by: answers 404 before 403, so a wrong owner cannot probe which ids exist](../../src/transport/routes/chats.test.ts#L85)).\nspecs/hal-engine-chat-routes/spec.md:20:- Owner ids are compared strictly, so numeric `1` and string `\"1\"` are different users rather than the same one ([validated by: compares owner ids strictly, so numeric 1 and string \"1\" are different users](../../src/transport/routes/chats.test.ts#L103)).\n----\n.specify/spec.md:5:HAL Engine is a generic, production-ready AI chat server framework designed to abstract away the complexity of building AI-powered chat backends. It provides a pluggable architecture for AI providers, tools, authentication, session management, and transport, allowing developers to focus on domain-specific logic rather than infrastructure plumbing.\n.specify/spec.md:7:The system handles core patterns including streaming responses, tool execution loops, WebSocket connections, session persistence, and message orchestration. It is structured as a layered application with clear separation of concerns from core types through infrastructure and transport.\n.specify/spec.md:28:- Dynamic tool registration via `ToolRegistry`\n.specify/spec.md:29:- JSON Schema tool definitions, declared per tool and forwarded to the model; the schema describes the input the model should produce and is NOT enforced before the executor runs\n.specify/spec.md:30:- Parallel tool execution within configurable tool loops\n.specify/spec.md:32:- Context-aware tool execution: the executor receives the caller's identity (`userId`, `sessionId`, `workspaceId`) and the auth headers the session carries, forwarded from the WebSocket upgrade or the HTTP chat request, not the conversation history\n.specify/spec.md:33:- Configurable maximum tool loop iterations (default: 5)\n.specify/spec.md:88:- `EntrySkipMessage`: Skip tool/response generation\n.specify/spec.md:106:- `AssistantEntry`: AI-generated response with tool calls\n.specify/spec.md:118:- `ToolCallContent`: Structured tool call representation\n.specify/spec.md:119:- `ToolResultContent`: Structured tool result representation\n.specify/spec.md:147:- Sets rate limits and tool execution constraints\n.specify/spec.md:154:- Registers domain-specific tools via `ToolRegistry`\n.specify/spec.md:165:- Observes tool execution through entry protocol\n.specify/spec.md:171:- Generates text responses and tool calls\n.specify/spec.md:174:- Validates tool calls against schemas\n.specify/spec.md:183:- Maximum tool loop iterations enforce termination (prevents infinite loops)\n.specify/spec.md:191:- Tool results must follow corresponding tool calls\n.specify/spec.md:192:- Thinking entries (when present) appear before tool calls or text\n.specify/spec.md:201:- User context is available to tools and infrastructure components\n.specify/spec.md:224:- Tool results are validated against original tool definition schema\n.specify/spec.md:245:4. **Error Rate**: Failed messages, tool execution errors, provider failures\n.specify/spec.md:249:1. **Tool Success Rate**: Percentage of tool calls that execute without error\n.specify/spec.md:255:1. **Time to Integration**: Hours to implement custom provider or tool\n.specify/spec.md:268:2. **Tool Execution Idempotency**: Safe to retry failed tool calls\n.specify/spec.md:281:- **Layer 4 (Orchestration)**: Chat orchestrator, tool registry, conversation context, entry management\n.specify/spec.md:298:- `tools`: Custom tool registry (defaults to empty)\n.specify/spec.md:302:- `orchestrator`: Max tool rounds, context config, lifecycle an absolute path is taken as given\";\n\nconst args = process.argv.slice(2);\nconst flags = args.filter((arg) => arg.startsWith(\"--\"));\nconst specArgs = args.filter((arg) => !arg.startsWith(\"--\"));\n\nfor (const flag of flags) {\n  if (flag === \"--json\") {\n    continue;\n  }\n  process.stderr.write(`${USAGE}\\n`);\n  process.exit(2);\n}\n\nconst asJson = flags.includes(\"--json\");\n\nconst discoverSpecs = () => {\n  const specsDir = join(root, \"specs\");\n  const slugs = readdirSync(specsDir, { withFileTypes: true })\n    .filter((entry) => entry.isDirectory())\n    .map((entry) => entry.name)\n    .sort();\n\n  return [...slugs.map((slug) => `specs/${slug}/spec.md`), \".specify/spec.md\"];\n};\n\nconst specPaths =\n  specArgs.length > 0\n    ? specArgs.map((path) => relative(root, resolve(root, path)))\n    : discoverSpecs();\n\nconst readSpec = (specPath) => {\n  try {\n    return readFileSync(join(root, specPath), \"utf8\");\n  } catch (error) {\n    process.stderr.write(`cannot read spec ${specPath}: ${error.message}\\n`);\n    process.exit(2);\n  }\n};\n\nconst findings = [];\nconst specsWithFindings = new Set();\nlet statementsScanned = 0;\n\nfor (const specPath of specPaths) {\n----\n# HAL Engine spec anchor drift\n\n| Field  | Value |\n| ------ | ----- |\n| Issue  | n/a   |\n| Status | In Progress |\n\nA citation written `([validated by](../../src/X.test.ts#Lnn))` names a line, and a line number is the least stable thing a file has. Insert eight lines above it and the anchor still resolves, still renders, and now points at something else entirely — the spec reads as cited while citing the wrong thing, and nothing about it looks wrong. `scripts/repoint-spec-anchors.mjs` closes that: it resolves each anchor to the content it cited in a baseline ref, finds that content in the working copy, and rewrites the number. `--check` is the CI gate that refuses drift; a plain run is the fix.\n\n## What counts as an anchor\n\nAny relative path with a file extension followed by `#L<n>`, in any spec under `specs/<slug>/spec.md` plus `.specify/spec.md`. A link whose fragment is not a line number is not an anchor and is left alone, however much the file around it moves ([validated by: leaves a link whose fragment is not a line number untouched](../../scripts/repoint-spec-anchors.test.ts#L506)). Test files are the common case, but nothing restricts it to them: a citation into a workflow, a config or a script is repointed the same way ([validated by: repoints docs, README and script citations alike](../../scripts/repoint-spec-anchors.test.ts#L454), [root config](../../scripts/repoint-spec-anchors.test.ts#L476), [workflow and manifest](../../scripts/repoint-spec-anchors.test.ts#L490)).\n\nThe label is not part of the anchor. A descriptive label such as `[validated by]` is never touched ([validated by: leaves a descriptive label alone while syncing a short-form one beside it](../../scripts/repoint-spec-anchors.test.ts#L420)). A short-form `[Lnn](../../src/X.test.ts#Lnn)` is different: its label carries no meaning beyond the number, so it is force-synced to whatever its own href says ([validated by: syncs labels even in a spec skipped for a differing anchor set](../../scripts/repoint-spec-anchors.test.ts#L399)). Syncing happens after the rewrite, so a repointed href carries its label with it in the same run ([validated by: carries the label along when the href is repointed](../../scripts/repoint-spec-anchors.test.ts#L354)).\n\n## The baseline is the point\n\nBaseline line numbers come from the base ref's copy of the **spec**, never from the working copy.\n\n- That is what makes a second run against the same baseline a no-op rather than a second translation ([validated by: is a no-op the second time, because the baseline is read from the base ref](../../scripts/repoint-spec-anchors.test.ts#L76)).\n- `base-ref` defaults to `origin/main` ([validated by: defaults the base ref to origin/main](../../scripts/repoint-spec-anchors.test.ts#L612)).\n\nThree cases have no baseline to reason from, and each is skipped rather than guessed:\n\n- A spec absent from the base ref is skipped whole. It is new, so no earlier line numbers exist to translate ([validated by: skips a spec absent from the base ref](../../scripts/repoint-spec-anchors.test.ts#L234)).\n- A spec whose ordered list of cited paths differs from the base ref's is skipped whole. Its anchors were authored against the working tree ([validated by: skips a spec whose ordered anchor paths differ from the base ref](../../scripts/repoint-spec-anchors.test.ts#L214)).\n- A single anchor whose line differs from the base spec's at the same position was deliberately retargeted by a spec edit. It is taken as authored, never rewritten, and counted under `retargeted (not checked)` ([validated by: takes a manually retargeted anchor as authored and never rewrites it](../../scripts/repoint-spec-anchors.test.ts#L246)).\n\nSkipping suspends only the baseline comparison. Rotten anchors are still reported in every one of those cases ([validated by: still checks a spec that was skipped for a differing anchor set](../../scripts/repoint-spec-anchors.test.ts#L326)).\n\n## Resolving a moved anchor\n\nThe content of the baseline line is looked up in the working copy of the same file.\n\n- Exactly one match is the answer ([validated by: moves each anchor to where the base ref's line content now lives](../../scripts/repoint-spec-anchors.test.ts#L64)).\n- Lines are compared with quotes and whitespace dropped, so a formatter pass over a cited file is not drift: the anchor follows the line it cited, and a file reformatted in place reads as up to date ([validated by: follows a line a formatter rewrote, because quotes and spacing are not drift](../../scripts/repoint-spec-anchors.test.ts#L651), [validated by: reads a file reformatted in place as up to date under --check](../../scripts/repoint-spec-anchors.test.ts#L664)).\n- No match is `unresolved`: the cited line is gone, and a person has to decide what the spec meant ([validated by: reports an anchor whose baseline content is gone as unresolved, and leaves it alone](../../scripts/repoint-spec-anchors.test.ts#L90)).\n- Several matches are scored against the four lines either side, and the candidate whose neighbourhood best reproduces the baseline's wins ([validated by: picks the occurrence whose surrounding lines match the baseline](../../scripts/repoint-spec-anchors.test.ts#L144)).\n- If two candidates score equally the anchor is `unresolved` as ambiguous. It is never a coin flip ([validated by: refuses to guess when the context ties, and rewrites nothing](../../scripts/repoint-spec-anchors.test.ts#L193)).\n- Two anchors citing identical content each resolve to their own occurrence rather than collapsing onto one, because every anchor is resolved from its own baseline line ([validated by: sends two identical assertions to their own occurrences](../../scripts/repoint-spec-anchors.test.ts#L152)).\n----\n50:        run: npm ci --ignore-scripts\n57:        run: npm run typecheck\n62:        run: npm run typecheck:example\n65: Rotten anchors are still reported in every one of those cases ([validated by: still checks a spec that was skipped for a differing anchor set](../../scripts/repoint-spec-anchors.test.ts#L326)).\n\n## Resolving a moved anchor\n\nThe content of the baseline line is looked up in the working copy of the same file.\n\n- Exactly one match is the answer ([validated by: moves each anchor to where the base ref's line content now lives](../../scripts/repoint-spec-anchors.test.ts#L64)).\n- Lines are compared with quotes and whitespace dropped, so a formatter pass over a cited file is not drift: the anchor follows the line it cited, and a file reformatted in place reads as up to date ([validated by: follows a line a formatter rewrote, because quotes and spacing are not drift](../../scripts/repoint-spec-anchors.test.ts#L651), [validated by: reads a file reformatted in place as up to date under --check](../../scripts/repoint-spec-anchors.test.ts#L664)).\n- No match is `unresolved`: the cited line is gone, and a person has to decide what the spec meant ([validated by: reports an anchor whose baseline content is gone as unresolved, and leaves it alone](../../scripts/repoint-spec-anchors.test.ts#L90)).\n- Several matches are scored against the four lines either side, and the candidate whose neighbourhood best reproduces the baseline's wins ([validated by: picks the occurrence whose surrounding lines match the baseline](../../scripts/repoint-spec-anchors.test.ts#L144)).\n- If two candidates score equally the anchor is `unresolved` as ambiguous. It is never a coin flip ([validated by: refuses to guess when the context ties, and rewrites nothing](../../scripts/repoint-spec-anchors.test.ts#L193)).\n- Two anchors citing identical content each resolve to their own occurrence rather than collapsing onto one, because every anchor is resolved from its own baseline line ([validated by: sends two identical assertions to their own occurrences](../../scripts/repoint-spec-anchors.test.ts#L152)).\n----\n50:        run: npm ci --ignore-scripts\n57:        run: npm run typecheck\n62:        run: npm run typecheck:example\n65:        run: npm run eslint\n80:        run: npm run lint:backlog\n83:        run: npm run prettier:check\n88:        run: npm run test:coverage\n91:        run: npm run build\n99:        run: node scripts/repoint-spec-anchors.mjs --check origin/main\n109:        run: npm run docs:check\n114:        run: npm run docs:check-paths\n117:        run: npm run docs:check-spikes\n120:        run: npm run check:regions\n125:        run: npm run spec:names\n128:        run: npm run check:spec-links\n136:        run: npm run check:spec-status\n147:        run: bash scripts/check-changelog.sh \"origin/${{ github.base_ref }}\"\n167:        run: npm ci --ignore-scripts\n172:        run: npm run check:audit\n177:        run: npm run check:action-pins\n198:        run: npm ci --ignore-scripts\n203:        run: npm run build\n209:        run: npm run smoke -- bare\n212:        run: npm run smoke -- full\n253:        run: |\n324:          # sessionId: 's1',\n    userId: 'u1',\n    entries: [{role: 'user', content, timestamp: SENT_AT}],\n  };\n}\n\nfunction createMockProvider(response: string, usage?: UsageMetadata): AIProvider {\n  return {\n    async *sendMessage(_params: SendMessageParams): AsyncGenerator<MessageChunk> {\n      yield {type: 'text', text: response};\n      yield {type: 'stop', stopReason: 'end_turn', usage};\n {\nindex.ts\nmockProvider.test.ts\nmockProvider.ts\nexport interface Logger {\n  debug(category: string, message: string, fields?: Record<string, unknown>): void;\n  info(category: string, message: string, fields?: Record<string, unknown>): void;\n  warn(category: string, message: string, fields?: Record<string, unknown>): void;\n  error(category: string, message: string, fields?: Record<string, unknown>): void;\n}\n\nconst LOG_LEVELS = {DEBUG: 0, INFO: 1, WARN: 2, ERROR: 3} as const;\ntype LevelName = keyof typeof LOG_LEVELS;\n\nconst METHODS = {DEBUG: 'debug', INFO: 'info', WARN: 'warn', ERROR: 'error'} as const;\n\nconst resolvedLevel = (() => {\n  const env = (process.env.LOG_LEVEL || 'info').toUpperCase();\n  return LOG_LEVELS[env as LevelName] ?? LOG_LEVELS.INFO;\n})();\n\nexport const UNSERIALISABLE = '[unserialisable]';\n\nfunction emit(level: LevelName, category: string, message: string, fields?: Record<string, unknown>): void {\n  // Caller fields are nested under `data`, so one named `severity` cannot overwrite the line's own.\n  const line = {\n    severity: level,\n    message,\n    timestamp: new Date().toISOString(),\n    category,\n    ...(fields ? {data: fields} : {}),\n  };\n\n  // ERROR to stderr: a stream that cannot be split by severity cannot be alerted on.\n  // eslint-disable-next-line no-console -- the one place this package writes to a stream\n  (level === 'ERROR' ? console.error : console.log)(serialise(line));\n}\n\n// A throw here would take down the call site being observed, so an unserialisable field costs that field alone.\nfunction serialise(line: Record<string, unknown>): string {\n  const whole = probe(() => JSON.stringify(line));\n  if (whole.ok) return whole.value;\n\n  // String() runs the caller's toString, so it is attempted inside the probe rather than before it.\n  const degraded = probe(() =>\n    JSON.stringify({\n      severity: line.severity,\n      message: String(line.message),\n      timestamp: line.timestamp,\n      category: String(line.category),\n      data: `${UNSERIALISABLE}: ${whole.reason}`,\n    })\n  );\n  if (degraded.ok) return degraded.value;\n\n  // Nothing the caller supplied survives into this one, so it cannot fail in turn.\n  return JSON.stringify({\n    severity: line.severity,\n    message: UNSERIALISABLE,\n    timestamp: line.timestamp,\n    category: UNSERIALISABLE,\n    data: `${UNSERIALISABLE}: ${degraded.reason}`,\n  });\n}\nexport {ToolRegistry, normalizeToolResponse} from './registry.js';\nexport type {ToolResponse, ToolContext, ToolExecutor, ToolDefinitionSource, RegisteredTool} from './registry.js';\n# Adding a New Tool\n\nTools let the AI model fetch data or perform actions during a conversation. When the model decides it needs information (like weather data or a database lookup), it invokes a tool by name, the server executes it, and the result feeds back into the conversation.\n\nAdding a new tool takes two steps: define it and register it. The rest is automatic -- the orchestrator passes tools to the AI provider, and the client already knows how to render any tool entry.\n\n## Step 1: Create the tool file\n\nCreate a new file in `src/orchestration/tools/`. Each tool exports two things:\n\n- A **`ToolDefinition`** describing the tool's name, purpose, and input schema\n- A **`ToolExecutor`** function that runs the tool and returns a JSON string\n\nHere is a complete example:\n\n<!-- doc-block: example/weather-tool.ts#weather-tool -->\n```typescript\nimport type {ToolDefinition, ToolExecutor} from '@re-cinq/hal-engine';\n\nexport const weatherTool: ToolDefinition = {\n  name: 'get_weather',\n  description:\n    'Get the current weather for a location.\\n\\n' +\n    'Use this tool when the user:\\n' +\n    '* Asks about weather or temperature\\n' +\n    '* Wants to know conditions at a specific place\\n' +\n    '* Needs a forecast for planning\\n\\n' +\n    'Returns temperature, conditions, humidity, and wind speed.',\n  promptInstructions:\n    'Use this when users ask about weather at a specific location. ' +\n    'Call this for questions like \"What is the weather in Berlin?\" or \"Is it raining in Tokyo?\"',\n  examplePrompts: ['What is the weather in Berlin?', 'Is it raining in Tokyo?'],\n  inputSchema: {\n    type: 'object',\n    properties: {\n      location: {\n        type: 'string',\n        description: 'The city name or location to look up.',\n      },\n      units: {\n        type: 'string',\n        enum: ['celsius', 'fahrenheit'],\n        description: 'Temperature units. Defaults to celsius.',\n      },\n    },\n    required: ['location'],\n  },\n};\n\nexport const executeWeather: ToolExecutor = async input => {\n  const location = (input.location as string) || 'Unknown';\n  const units = (input.units as string) || 'celsius';\n\n  // Replace with a real API call. `input` is untrusted: inputSchema shapes what the model sends, it does not enforce it.\n  const result = {\n    location,\n    temperature: 18,\n    units,\n    conditions: 'Partly cloudy',\n    humidity: 65,\n    windSpeed: 12,\n    windUnit: 'km/h',\n  };\n\n  return JSON.stringify(result);\n};\n```\n\n### Writing a good tool description\n\nThe `description` field is what the AI model reads to decide when to use the tool. Be specific:\n\n- List the situations where the tool should be used (\"Use this tool when...\")\n- Describe what the tool returns\n- Give example queries if it helps\n\nThe more precise the description, the better the model's judgment about when to invoke it.\n\n### Adding prompt ' +\n    'Call this for questions like \"What is the weather in Berlin?\" or \"Is it raining in Tokyo?\"',\n  examplePrompts: ['What is the weather in Berlin?', 'Is it raining in Tokyo?'],\n  inputSchema: {\n    type: 'object',\n    properties: {\n      location: {\n        type: 'string',\n        description: 'The city name or location to look up.',\n      },\n      units: {\n        type: 'string',\n        enum: ['celsius', 'fahrenheit'],\n        description: 'Temperature units. Defaults to celsius.',\n      },\n    },\n    required: ['location'],\n  },\n};\n\nexport const executeWeather: ToolExecutor = async input => {\n  const location = (input.location as string) || 'Unknown';\n  const units = (input.units as string) || 'celsius';\n\n  // Replace with a real API call. `input` is untrusted: inputSchema shapes what the model sends, it does not enforce it.\n  const result = {\n    location,\n    temperature: 18,\n    units,\n    conditions: 'Partly cloudy',\n    humidity: 65,\n    windSpeed: 12,\n    windUnit: 'km/h',\n  };\n\n  return JSON.stringify(result);\n};\n```\n\n### Writing a good tool description\n\nThe `description` field is what the AI model reads to decide when to use the tool. Be specific:\n\n- List the situations where the tool should be used (\"Use this tool when...\")\n- Describe what the tool returns\n- Give example queries if it helps\n\nThe more precise the description, the better the model's judgment about when to invoke it.\n\n### Adding prompt instructions","stderr":"","interrupted":false,"isImage":false,"noOutputExpected":false},"tool_result_meta":[{"id":"toolu_018ScxFWm1jXKMLNT1RffBB3","permission_decision":{"decision":"accept","source":"config","reason_type":"mode"}}]}
